## Safeguarding against drug and trial information leakage during LLM inference



To ensure that the language model did not inadvertently use drug- or trial-related information during inference, we implemented a keyword detection system. This document displays ±100 words of context around each keyword match to enable manual review of flagged passages. We applied case-insensitive, whole-word regular-expression matching to all evidence summaries. Below, we (i) provide the keyword dictionary, (ii) describe the categories of keyword matches, and (iii) present the manual review of all cases.


## Keyword dictionary

### Drug-related terms

| Category | Keywords |
|----------|----------|
| **General** | drug, drugs, medication, medications, therapy, therapies, therapeutic, therapeutics, pharmaceutical, pharmaceuticals, pharmacotherapy, medicinal product |
| **Dosing and administration** | dose, doses, dosing, mg/kg, mg/day, mg, tablet, tablets, capsule, capsules, intravenous, infusion, subcutaneous, injection, injections |
| **Regulatory and development status** | investigational drug, investigational agent, investigational, clinical candidate, approved drug, approved therapy, fda-approved, ema-approved, on-label, off-label |

### Trial-related terms

| Category | Keywords |
|----------|----------|
| **General trial terminology** | trial, trials, clinical trial, clinical trials, clinical study, clinical studies, interventional study |
| **Study design** | randomized, randomised, placebo, placebo-controlled, placebo controlled, double-blind, double blind, single-blind, single blind, open-label, open label, crossover, cross-over, parallel group, parallel-group |
| **Trial phases** | phase i, phase 1, phase-1, phase ii, phase 2, phase-2, phase iii, phase 3, phase-3, phase iv, phase 4, phase-4, phase i/ii, phase 1/2, phase ii/iii, phase 2/3, first-in-human, first in human, proof-of-concept, proof of concept |
| **Dose-finding terminology** | dose-escalation, dose escalation, dose-expansion, dose expansion, maximum tolerated dose, mtd, recommended phase 2 dose, rp2d |
| **Registry** | clinicaltrials.gov |
| **Endpoints and statistics** | primary endpoint, secondary endpoint, overall survival, progression-free survival, progression free survival, pfs, hazard ratio, response rate, objective response |
| **Miscellaneous trial language** | study arm, treatment arm, trial arm, multicenter, multicentre, interventional, prospective cohort, randomized trial, sponsor, investigator-initiated |

## Categories of Keyword Matches

**Category 1: Non-therapeutic or drug development context**

Legitimate biological terminology unrelated to therapeutic contexts. Terms such as "dose" describe gene expression sensitivity rather than drug administration, and "clinical candidate" refers to candidate-gene association studies rather than investigational compounds.

Example 1: ANGPTL3 Dyslipidemias Very Strong Biallelic loss-of-function variants in ANGPTL3 cause familial combined hypolipidemia (FHBL2), characterized by markedly reduced triglycerides, LDL-C, and HDL-C; heterozygous carriers show intermediate lipid lowering (**dose** response).

Example 2: However, large GWAS have not implicated FAAH at genome-wide significance for social anxiety disorder specifically, and some **clinical candidate**-gene studies have been null/underpowered.

**Category 2: Model self-reflection**

Instances where the model explicitly demonstrates awareness of leakage concerns and actively excludes potentially confounding information.


Example 1: Reports that examine ABCB1 variants vis-à-vis transplant outcomes are often **therapy**-contextual and inconsistent; they are not counted to avoid confounding. Thus, ICES is low (1.8), and the verdict is Insufficient.

Example 2:  In line with leakage control, **therapy**-response literature related to ABCB1 was not used.

**Category 3: Non-interventional references**

References to clinical studies that are not interventional drug trials. These typically include descriptive studies of disease pathophysiology or interventional studies conducted in animal models (e.g., mice) rather than humans (both are harmless and pose no leakage risk).

Example 1: Multiple ***clinical studies*** report elevated LTB4 in COPD airway samples, consistent with increased pathway activity [AJRCCM 2003; review JACI 2016]. Animal studies further support a role for the 5-LO–LTB4 axis in emphysema-relevant inflammation, although most results are pathway-level and not Alox5ap-specific [Nat Med 2010; MGI].

Example 2:  Genetic and ***interventional*** studies in murine autoimmune models indicate that the B7–CD28 costimulatory axis is required for full disease expression; CD80 contributes alongside CD86, with partial redundancy.

#### Summary of Category Frequencies

| Category | Description | Count | Percentage |
|----------|-------------|-------|------------|
| 1 | Non-therapeutic or drug development context | 177 | 70.0% |
| 2 | Model self-reflection | 35 | 13.8% |
| 3 | Non-interventional references | 41 | 16.2% |
| **Total** | | **253** | **100%** |

## Manual review

We use **bold** to denote “drug-related keywords” and ***bold italic*** to denote “trial-related keywords.” We manually reviewed all 253 cases and assigned each to one of several categories, including “non-therapeutic or drug-development context,” “model self-reflection,” and “non-interventional references.” Below, we list all cases.

---

## ABCB1-D001943

**Drug keywords:** drug

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

> ...canonical oncogenic or DNA repair pathways. GTEx v8 shows relatively low ABCB1 expression in normal breast mammary tissue compared with barrier tissues (e.g., intestine, liver, brain endothelial cells), arguing against a strong cell-autonomous role in mammary epithelium. Human Protein Atlas reports low-to-variable expression of ABCB1 in normal breast tissue; no consistent tumor-type specific enrichment pattern that would, by itself, suggest a causal role in breast tumor initiation. Mouse orthology is one-to-many (Abcb1a/Abcb1b). IMPC phenotyping of Abcb1a or Abcb1b disruption shows pharmacokinetic/barrier-related phenotypes; no spontaneous mammary tumor predisposition reported, limiting translatability to human breast cancer etiology. Abcb1b knockout phenotypes similarly emphasize **drug**/xenobiotic transport roles; mammary neoplasia is not a consistent phenotype in these models. Across human germline genetics (GWAS Catalog; BCAC meta-analyses), ABCB1 is not implicated as a breast cancer susceptibility gene. Somatic driver compendia also do not list ABCB1 as a recurrently selected driver in breast tumors, and functional expression context in normal breast is low. Animal models with split orthology do not show mammary tumor phenotypes. Overall, evidence lines are coherently non-supportive; direction of effect is unknown. Pan-cancer driver gene surveys do not include ABCB1 among confidently identified driver genes, including in breast cancer cohorts, supporting the absence of a...

## ABCB1-D006086

**Drug keywords:** therapy

**Category:** Model self-reflection

**Match 1:**
> ...placement supports potential impact on mucosal barrier and immune cell exposure to environmental ligands. Mouse Abcb1a (mdr1a) knockout models develop spontaneous colitis resembling human ulcerative colitis on permissive backgrounds, indicating a role for ABCB1-related transport in intestinal immune homeostasis; however, this is not a GVHD model and mouse-to-human orthology is 1-to-2 (Abcb1a/Abcb1b), reducing direct translatability. Across data types, ABCB1 biology coheres with tissues central to GVHD (especially gut epithelium), and animal loss-of-function yields intestinal inflammation. However, there is a lack of direct, replicated human genetic association with GVHD, and candidate pharmaco-genetic reports (not counted here) have been inconsistent and potentially **therapy**-dependent. Overall, cross-line agreement on a causal, human-relevant ABCB1→GVHD relationship is limited. ABCB1 encodes P-glycoprotein, an ATP-dependent efflux transporter enriched in intestinal epithelium and other barrier tissues, with detectable immune-cell expression, aligning with GVHD target organs and pathophysiology (gut, liver, skin) (HPA; GTEx; UniProt). In mouse, Abcb1a knockout causes spontaneous colitis, supporting a role in intestinal immune homeostasis, although this phenotype is not a graft-versus-host model and mouse orthology is split (Abcb1a/Abcb1b), limiting direct inference to human GVHD (MGI). Despite biological plausibility, we did not identify robust human genetic evidence (GWAS/credible sets, colocalized QTLs, or rare-variant burden) directly implicating ABCB1 in GVHD risk. Some literature explores ABCB1 variants and transplant outcomes, but these are often **therapy**-contextual and inconsistent; they are not counted here to avoid treatment-dependent confounding. Given absent human genetic anchors and only indirect functional/animal support, the overall causal, human-relevant link between ABCB1 and GVHD is currently insufficient. Graft-versus-host disease (catalog search) No genome-wide significant ABCB1 associations for GVHD identified as of latest catalog releases. Locus-to-gene credible sets (cross-trait) No OTG credible set linking ABCB1 to GVHD located (site-wide search). GVHD pathophysiology/organ targets Overview of GVHD mechanisms and target tissues; does not identify ABCB1 as a robust GVHD genetic locus. Tissue and cell-type expression High expression in intestinal epithelium, liver, barrier tissues; immune cell expression...

**Match 2:**
> ...within ABC transporter pathways; supports barrier/xenobiotic transport roles. Mouse Abcb1a (mdr1a) knockout phenotypes Spontaneous colitis phenotype; not a GVHD model; human–mouse orthology is 1-to-2 (Abcb1a/Abcb1b). Current evidence does not support a causal, human-relevant ABCB1→GVHD relationship. No replicated human genetic associations (GWAS/credible sets/rare variants) implicate ABCB1 in GVHD (GWAS Catalog; Open Targets Genetics). ABCB1 shows relevant expression in GVHD target organs and immune cells (HPA; GTEx), and mouse Abcb1a knockout yields intestinal inflammation (MGI), which is pathophysiologically adjacent to gut GVHD but not specific for GVHD and complicated by paralogous orthology. Reports that examine ABCB1 variants vis-à-vis transplant outcomes are often **therapy**-contextual and inconsistent; they are not counted to avoid confounding. Thus, ICES is low (1.8), and the verdict is Insufficient.

## ABCB1-D007938

**Drug keywords:** therapy

**Category:** Model self-reflection

> ...perturbations show barrier/xenobiotic phenotypes; no leukemia noted. Mouse genetics/phenotypes (Abcb1a, Abcb1b) No reported spontaneous leukemia phenotypes attributable to Abcb1 loss. Current curated human genetics does not implicate ABCB1 in leukemia susceptibility or pathogenesis: GWAS Catalog lists no ABCB1–leukemia associations, and leukemia GWAS trait pages do not map the locus to ABCB1. Somatic driver resources (IntOGen, Cancer Gene Census) do not identify ABCB1 as a leukemia driver gene. Functional genomics shows low expression in blood/immune cells (GTEx, HPA), and animal models do not show leukemic phenotypes with Abcb1 perturbation. Direction of effect and cell-of-action remain unclear. In line with leakage control, **therapy**-response literature related to ABCB1 was not used.

## ACE-D009203

**Drug keywords:** infusion

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

> ...integral to atherothrombosis and MI. Strong cis-pQTLs for circulating ACE support that genetic variation modulates ACE abundance/activity in humans, aligning with a plausible exposure axis to impact MI-related biology even if MI association itself is not robust. Mouse Ace knockouts and disruptions show marked hypotension and vascular/renal phenotypes, consistent with reduced systemic RAS activity; these phenotypes align mechanistically with protection from hypertensive vascular injury that contributes to MI risk. Systematic mouse phenotyping confirms decreased systolic blood pressure and cardiovascular-system alterations when Ace is perturbed, with strong orthology to human ACE. In atherosclerosis-prone mouse models, heightened RAS signaling (e.g., Ang II **infusion**) accelerates atherogenesis and vascular inflammation, supporting the mechanistic axis whereby increased ACE/Ang II activity promotes atherothrombotic processes related to MI. Mechanistic biology and animal models consistently support that increased ACE/Ang II activity would increase atherothrombotic risk. However, human genetic association of ACE with MI per se is not robust in modern GWAS (despite strong ACE pQTLs and early candidate-gene signals), leading to overall mixed strength: coherent mechanism with limited direct MI genetics. ACE occupies a central enzymatic position in the RAS, with clear expression in vascular/cardiac-relevant cells, and functional roles that plausibly influence MI pathophysiology (vasoconstriction, inflammation, remodeling) (Reactome; GTEx/HPA)....

## ACHE-D009157

**Drug keywords:** mg

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

**Match 1:**
> ACHE Myasthenia Gravis Weak Across curated GWAS resources, ACHE is not implicated as a risk locus for autoimmune myasthenia gravis (most replicated signals map to HLA class II, PTPN22, CTLA4, TNIP1 and others; ACHE does not appear among reported **MG** risk genes). Open Targets Genetics gene- and locus-level summaries do not prioritize ACHE for myasthenia gravis based on credible sets or locus-to-gene scoring; no fine-mapped **MG** variants colocalize with ACHE regulatory features. Reviews synthesizing **MG** genetics emphasize HLA and immune-regulatory genes and do not report ACHE as a replicated susceptibility gene for **MG**. ACHE encodes acetylcholinesterase, the principal enzyme that hydrolyzes acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction; increased enzymatic activity shortens acetylcholine lifetime, decreased activity prolongs it—directly modulating neuromuscular transmission. Tissue expression and protein localization data show ACHE is present in skeletal muscle and enriched at the neuromuscular junction (anchored via COLQ in the synaptic basal lamina), supporting relevant cells of action for **MG**. GTEx confirms appreciable ACHE transcript in skeletal muscle and nervous system tissues, aligning with neuromuscular synaptic function implicated in **MG**. Pathway annotations place ACHE centrally in cholinergic synaptic transmission and acetylcholine degradation (Reactome/GO), providing strong mechanistic coherence to neuromuscular junction physiology perturbed in **MG**. In **MG**, pathogenic autoantibodies reduce functional acetylcholine receptor numbers and alter endplate structure; in this context, enzymes that set acetylcholine lifetime in the cleft (ACHE) are expected to modify synaptic efficacy and symptom expression. Ache knockout mice show severe cholinergic dysfunction and perinatal lethality with profound neuromuscular abnormalities, demonstrating ACHE’s essential role in neuromuscular transmission (orthologous function aligns with human NMJ physiology although not **MG**-specific). Transgenic/conditional manipulations of acetylcholinesterase levels in mice alter neuromuscular transmission and muscle function, supporting a causal role for ACHE dosage in endplate physiology relevant to **MG** biology. Human congenital endplate acetylcholinesterase deficiency (usually due to COLQ, the ACHE anchoring protein) causes a myasthenic phenotype, underscoring that perturbing AChE function at the NMJ—whether by enzyme or anchoring—can lead to fatigable weakness. Lines of evidence agree that ACHE is central to neuromuscular synaptic physiology and that altering its function changes neuromuscular transmission. However, large-scale human genetics for autoimmune **MG** do not implicate ACHE as a risk gene, and no fine-mapped **MG**-eQTL colocalizations or rare variant burdens implicate ACHE directly. Animal and congenital data confirm relevance of ACHE to NMJ function but are not specific to autoimmune **MG** etiology. Overall, evidence is mechanistically coherent but lacks human genetic causality for **MG**. ACHE is a key enzyme of the neuromuscular junction that hydrolyzes acetylcholine, with well-established expression and localization in skeletal muscle endplates and strong pathway placement in cholinergic synaptic transmission (PMID:8042853; PMID:25613900; Reactome R-HSA-159596). Animal models and human congenital disorders affecting endplate AChE function demonstrate that perturbations of AChE levels or anchoring profoundly affect neuromuscular transmission and lead to myasthenic phenotypes (MGI:87851; PMID:15922100). In autoimmune myasthenia gravis, pathogenic mechanisms reduce effective acetylcholine receptor function, so AChE activity is expected to modify synaptic efficacy and symptom expression (PMID:30814604). Nevertheless, contemporary human genetic studies of **MG** do not implicate ACHE as a susceptibility gene, and there are no replicated fine-mapped loci, colocalized regulatory signals, or rare variant burdens linking ACHE to **MG** risk (GWAS Catalog; Open Targets Genetics). Thus, the relationship is biologically plausible and supported by functional alignment but lacks direct human genetic causality for **MG**, warranting a Weak causal, human-relevant verdict. Myasthenia gravis (trait-level GWAS summary) No reported **MG** associations mapping to ACHE among cataloged loci; HLA and immune-regulatory loci dominate. Gene-level locus-to-gene prioritization ACHE gene page; no **MG** credible set or prioritized association reported. Myasthenia gravis pathophysiology Comprehensive review of **MG** etiology (autoantibody-mediated); does not list ACHE as **MG** susceptibility gene. Myasthenia gravis genetics Emphasizes HLA and immune-regulatory risk loci; no ACHE implication. Neuromuscular junction; cholinergic synapse Classic review of cholinesterases, substrate kinetics, and NMJ biology; supports DoE inference (AChE hydrolyzes ACh). Skeletal muscle; peripheral nerve Human Protein Atlas gene page; protein localization and RNA expression supporting NMJ relevance. GTEx multi-tissue expression Confirms ACHE expression in skeletal muscle and nervous system; provides tissue context. Cholinergic synapse pathway Reactome pathway placing ACHE in acetylcholine hydrolysis. Neuromuscular junction structural biology Demonstrates anchoring of ACHE at NMJ via COLQ, reinforcing cell-of-action. Mouse Ache knockout/alleles MGI summary: Ache loss alters neuromuscular function; perinatal...

**Match 2:**
> ...pathway Reactome pathway placing ACHE in acetylcholine hydrolysis. Neuromuscular junction structural biology Demonstrates anchoring of ACHE at NMJ via COLQ, reinforcing cell-of-action. Mouse Ache knockout/alleles MGI summary: Ache loss alters neuromuscular function; perinatal lethality in null mutants. Congenital myasthenic syndromes; endplate AChE deficiency Shows clinical myasthenic phenotypes from impaired endplate AChE function (typically via COLQ). There is strong mechanistic and anatomical coherence that ACHE modulates neuromuscular junction signaling relevant to myasthenia gravis. ACHE is expressed and localized to the endplate, and altering its function in animal models or via anchoring defects produces myasthenic physiology. However, modern human genetic studies in **MG** do not implicate ACHE as a susceptibility gene, and no fine-mapped or colocalized regulatory signals tie ACHE variation to **MG** risk. Direction of effect inferred from physiology is that increased ACHE activity reduces synaptic acetylcholine and would be expected to exacerbate neuromuscular transmission failure in **MG**, while decreased ACHE prolongs signaling; yet congenital endplate AChE deficiency can itself cause myasthenic weakness, indicating that deviations in either direction impair NMJ function. Overall, the relationship is biologically plausible but lacks direct human genetic causality; hence a Weak verdict. Key sources: GWAS Catalog (accessed 2024), Open Targets Genetics, GTEx (PMID:32913098), HPA (PMID:25613900), Taylor & Radić (PMID:8042853), Engel et al. on CMS (PMID:15922100), **MG** pathogenesis review (PMID:30814604).

## ADORA1-D006949

**Drug keywords:** drug

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

> ...adipose antilipolysis IUPHAR adenosine receptor classification; antilipolytic actions via A1 receptor in adipocytes are discussed. Adenosine receptors—mechanisms and physiology Summarizes A1 receptor Gi coupling and inhibitory effects on lipolysis. Mouse genetics resource (MGI) No curated, reproducible Adora1 knockout plasma lipid phenotypes identified during this review. Human genetic evidence linking ADORA1 to hyperlipidemias is currently lacking: neither GWAS Catalog nor major GLGC studies implicate ADORA1, and there are no curated monogenic dyslipidemia assertions (GWAS Catalog gene page; Willer 2013, Nat Genet; GenCC). Mechanistically, ADORA1 reduces cAMP and lipolysis in adipocytes (Fredholm et al., Pharmacol Rev 2001; Chen et al., Nat Rev **Drug** Discov 2013), and ADORA1 is expressed in adipose (GTEx, HPA), providing biological plausibility. However, without fine-mapped GWAS/TWAS/coloc or replicated rare-variant associations, and without aligned, curated animal plasma-lipid phenotypes, the evidence does not currently support a causal, human-relevant relationship for hyperlipidemias. Direction of effect on plasma lipids remains uncertain in humans.

## ADRA2A-D004409

**Drug keywords:** drug

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

> ADRA2A Dyskinesia, **Drug**-Induced Insufficient No genome-wide significant or fine-mapped human genetic associations implicating ADRA2A in **drug**-induced dyskinesias (including tardive dyskinesia or levodopa-induced dyskinesia) are listed on the GWAS Catalog ADRA2A gene page. GWAS Catalog searches for 'tardive dyskinesia' and 'levodopa-induced dyskinesia' do not report loci implicating ADRA2A as of the latest releases. Open Targets Genetics gene summary for ADRA2A shows no colocalized GWAS signals or credible sets linking ADRA2A to **drug**-induced dyskinesia phenotypes. Gene-based rare variant burden resources (GeneBass) do not report published associations between rare ADRA2A variants and **drug**-induced dyskinesia phenotypes. ADRA2A shows neuronal expression in human brain, with appreciable RNA levels across cortical regions and basal ganglia, supporting CNS relevance. Human Protein Atlas reports ADRA2A expression in brain (neuronal enriched) with detectable RNA in basal ganglia, aligning with movement circuitry implicated in dyskinesias. ADRA2A is a Gi/o-coupled adrenergic GPCR that inhibits adenylate cyclase and modulates neurotransmitter release; these pathways are mechanistically relevant to synaptic plasticity changes underlying hyperkinetic movement phenotypes. Cis-eQTLs for ADRA2A are observed in GTEx brain tissues (e.g., cortex), indicating genetic regulation within relevant CNS contexts, though no colocalization with **drug**-induced dyskinesia traits has been established. Mouse Adra2a knockout models display altered behavioral phenotypes including changes in locomotor activity and stress-related behaviors; however, a direct genetic test of Adra2a in pharmacologically induced dyskinesia models has not been demonstrated. IMPC/MousePhenotype resources catalog behavioral and physiological abnormalities in Adra2a mutant mice, but do not report **drug**-induced dyskinesia phenotypes or definitive movement disorder homologs. Across human genetics resources (GWAS Catalog, Open Targets Genetics, rare variant burden resources), there is no reproducible association between ADRA2A and **drug**-induced dyskinesias. Functional genomics supports CNS expression and plausible synaptic roles, and mouse knockouts show behavioral changes, but no line directly links ADRA2A perturbation to **drug**-induced dyskinesia phenotypes. No explicit contradicting human genetic studies were identified; the main signal is absence of genetic association. Human genetic anchors linking ADRA2A to **drug**-induced dyskinesia are lacking: neither GWAS Catalog nor Open Targets Genetics report associations or fine-mapped signals implicating ADRA2A in tardive dyskinesia or levodopa-induced dyskinesia. Rare variant burden resources similarly do not report gene-level associations. Biological coherence is moderate given strong neuronal expression in cortex and basal ganglia (GTEx, HPA) and the receptor’s role in Gi/o-coupled modulation of neurotransmitter release (Reactome), which is relevant to dyskinesia-related circuit plasticity. Animal models of Adra2a disruption exhibit behavioral alterations, but there is no direct genetic demonstration of effects on pharmacologically induced dyskinesia outcomes. Overall, multiple lines of evidence do not converge on a causal, human-relevant relationship at present. The direction of effect and key cells of action for **drug**-induced dyskinesia remain undetermined for ADRA2A. Dyskinesia (general); gene-centric associations No ADRA2A associations reported for tardive dyskinesia or levodopa-induced dyskinesia. Tardive dyskinesia Search did not identify ADRA2A-implicating loci. Levodopa-induced dyskinesia Search did not identify ADRA2A-implicating loci. Gi/o-coupled GPCR signaling; synaptic modulation ADRA2A participates in G alpha (i) signaling and regulation of neurotransmitter release. Mouse Adra2a knockout phenotypes Behavioral phenotypes reported; no direct **drug**-induced dyskinesia phenotypes documented. Mouse Adra2a phenotyping Cataloged abnormalities; no explicit dyskinesia phenotypes. There is currently no convergent human genetic evidence that variation in ADRA2A causally influences susceptibility to **drug**-induced dyskinesias. Gene-centric and trait-centric queries in GWAS Catalog and Open Targets Genetics do not implicate ADRA2A, and rare-variant burden resources are also negative. Nevertheless, expression and pathway data (GTEx, HPA, Reactome) support biological plausibility given the receptor’s neuronal expression and synaptic modulatory role in CNS circuits relevant to movement control. Mouse Adra2a mutants show behavioral changes, but no studies were found that directly link genetic perturbation of Adra2a to pharmacologically induced dyskinesia outcomes, limiting translatability. The direction of effect and key cells of action remain undetermined. Taken together, the evidence is insufficient for a causal, human-relevant link for this T–I...

## ADRA2B-D004409

**Drug keywords:** drug

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

**Match 1:**
> ADRA2B Dyskinesia, **Drug**-Induced Insufficient Across curated human genetics resources, no genome-wide association signals or mapped loci implicating ADRA2B in **drug**-induced dyskinesias (including tardive dyskinesia and levodopa-induced dyskinesia) were identified. A separate search for levodopa-induced dyskinesia in the GWAS Catalog similarly yielded no associations implicating ADRA2B. ClinGen/GenCC contains no curated gene–disease validity assertions linking ADRA2B to movement disorders or dyskinesias. GTEx shows ADRA2B expression is highest in adrenal gland and arterial/vascular tissues, with low-to-undetectable expression across brain regions relevant to dyskinesia (e.g., caudate, putamen, nucleus accumbens), reducing biological plausibility for a direct neuronal/striatal role. Human Protein Atlas tissue and brain data indicate ADRA2B is enriched in vascular smooth muscle and peripheral tissues, with low neuronal expression; in contrast, paralogs ADRA2A and...

**Match 2:**
> ...regions relevant to motor control. Comparison within the alpha-2 adrenergic receptor family supports that ADRA2A and ADRA2C are more brain-expressed than ADRA2B, consistent with limited ADRA2B involvement in striatal circuits. ADRA2B is a Gi/o-coupled alpha-2 adrenergic receptor in canonical GPCR signaling pathways (GO/Reactome), establishing general mechanistic plausibility for neuromodulation but not a specific link to dyskinesia. Adra2b knockout mice display cardiovascular and vascular phenotypes (e.g., hypotension, impaired vasoconstriction) without reported movement or dyskinesia-like phenotypes, offering no aligned animal support for a causal role in dyskinesia. Across data types, there is a consistent lack of human genetic association for ADRA2B with **drug**-induced dyskinesias, low expression of ADRA2B in human striatal/brain tissues (GTEx, HPA), and animal knockout phenotypes that do not map to dyskinesia classes. No explicit refuting human studies were found; instead the evidence is largely absent or biologically mismatched. Animal genetics (Btk-deficient mice) demonstrate impaired B-cell development/antibody production, but **MG**-specific Btk genetic models were not identified in curated repositories. The likely direction-of-effect, inferred from immunology, is that decreased BTK activity would reduce **MG** risk/severity by diminishing autoantibody generation, yet this remains a mechanistic inference without human allelic support. X-chromosome underanalysis may partly explain absent signals, but until human genetic anchors or **MG**-specific animal validations emerge, the integrated causal evidence is best graded as weak. Key sources: GWAS Catalog, Open Targets Genetics, GeneBass, GTEx, Human Protein Atlas, Reactome, NEJM review (Gilhus 2016), and IMPC.

## C5-D009157

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

**Drug keywords:** mg

> C5 Myasthenia Gravis Strong Curated GWAS resources list multiple genome-wide significant loci for myasthenia gravis (e.g., HLA class II, PTPN22, CTLA4, TNFRSF11A), but do not implicate C5; no credible set or fine-mapped **MG** signal maps to C5. Open Targets Genetics does not report **MG** credible sets colocalizing with or prioritizing C5 as a causal gene; no **MG** locus-level V2G prioritization for C5. Human AChR antibody–positive **MG** shows complement activation at the neuromuscular junction with deposition of the terminal complement complex (C5b–9) on endplates, consistent with C5 activation being a key step in tissue injury. Clinical-pathophysiological overviews of **MG** emphasize that pathogenic AChR autoantibodies are largely IgG1/IgG3, fix complement, and drive MAC-mediated postsynaptic damage, placing C5 activation centrally in the mechanism. C5 encodes the precursor of C5a and C5b; C5b initiates MAC assembly (C5b–9) in the complement cascade, which mechanistically fits the observed endplate injury in **MG**. C5 is secreted primarily by hepatocytes and circulates in plasma; expression resources show high liver expression with systemic distribution, consistent with serum complement acting at the neuromuscular junction. Protein-level resources show C5 as a secreted plasma protein with highest expression in liver and presence in blood, aligning with a circulating effector role at the NMJ. In experimental autoimmune **MG** (EAMG) and passive-transfer **MG** models, disease is complement-dependent; genetic or experimental abrogation of terminal complement (including steps requiring C5 cleavage) prevents MAC formation and protects the neuromuscular junction. Complement’s terminal pathway is necessary for antibody-mediated endplate injury in animal **MG** models; complement deficiency at or downstream of C5 is protective in multiple species, consistent with a causal role for C5-mediated MAC formation. Across human pathology (C5b–9 on endplates), mechanism (C5 cleavage to C5a/C5b and MAC formation), and animal models (terminal complement required for EAMG), evidence is coherent for complement/C5-mediated postsynaptic damage in AChR+ **MG**. A notable nuance is **MG** heterogeneity: MuSK antibody–positive **MG** involves predominantly IgG4 autoantibodies, which do not fix complement, indicating the C5/MAC mechanism is subtype-specific rather than universal. C5 sits at the nexus of the terminal complement cascade: its cleavage yields C5a and C5b, with C5b initiating MAC (C5b–9). In AChR antibody–positive myasthenia gravis, human muscle studies show MAC deposition on neuromuscular junction endplates and morphological features of complement-mediated injury, directly implicating the C5-dependent terminal pathway in tissue damage (Conti-Fine et al., 2006; Gilhus, 2016). Animal **MG** models corroborate this: complement-dependent endplate injury is necessary for weakness, and genetic/experimental interruption of the terminal pathway (i.e., requiring C5 activation) abrogates disease, aligning directionally with lowered C5 activity reducing severity (reviews summarizing primary EAMG studies). While this provides a strong mechanistic and functional case, large-scale human genetic studies have not identified C5 as an **MG** risk locus, so gene–trait causality from human genetics is limited. Importantly, complement involvement is subtype-specific: it is strong in AChR+ **MG** but not expected in MuSK-IgG4 **MG**, which does not fix complement (Klooster et al., 2012). Considering the convergence of human pathophysiology and animal models, but limited direct human genetic association, the overall causal, human-relevant link between C5 activity and AChR+ **MG** pathogenesis is strong. Myasthenia gravis (GWAS Catalog trait page) Curated GWAS associations for **MG**; no reported association at the C5 locus. Open Targets Genetics: C5 gene page No **MG** credible sets or locus-to-gene prioritizations implicating C5 as of latest release. Complement cascade (terminal pathway includes C5 cleavage to MAC) C5 is required for C5a generation and C5b-9 MAC assembly. Expression—liver and plasma (secreted) C5 highly expressed in liver; consistent with secreted plasma protein acting systemically. Protein expression/localization Secreted complement protein; strong liver expression and presence in blood. Myasthenia gravis—pathophysiology JCI review summarizing human endplate MAC deposition and complement dependence in animal **MG** models. Myasthenia gravis—clinical and mechanistic overview NEJM review; AChR-IgG1/IgG3 complement fixing antibodies and MAC-mediated postsynaptic injury discussed. Myasthenia gravis—mechanisms and models Lancet Neurol review summarizing complement’s role in animal **MG** models and human pathology. MuSK antibody–positive **MG** JCI paper showing MuSK-IgG4 antibodies do not fix complement, highlighting subtype specificity of complement/C5 involvement. Overall, human genetic association between C5 and **MG** is limited or absent in curated GWAS resources, so HGC is low. However, strong, convergent biological coherence and functional evidence support a causal role for C5 in AChR+ **MG**: patient muscle shows C5b–9 (MAC) deposition on endplates, and animal **MG** models are complement-dependent, with terminal complement steps (requiring C5) necessary for neuromuscular junction injury (Conti-Fine et al., JCI 2006; Meriggioli & Sanders, Lancet Neurol 2009; Gilhus, NEJM 2016). Mechanistically, C5 cleavage to C5a/C5b and MAC assembly (Reactome) explains the observed pathology. Importantly, the complement/C5 mechanism is subtype-specific: MuSK-IgG4 **MG** typically does not fix complement (Klooster et al., JCI 2012). Integrating these lines, decreased C5 activity is expected to reduce risk/severity in AChR-antibody–mediated **MG**. The integrated causal evidence score supports a Strong verdict driven by pathophysiological and animal-model coherence, with a deduction due to the paucity of direct human genetic associations.

## CD3E-D009157

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

> CD3E Myasthenia Gravis Weak Across curated GWAS resources for myasthenia gravis (**MG**), multiple reproducible susceptibility loci are reported (notably HLA class I/II and several immune-regulatory loci), but no genome-wide significant association at or near the CD3E locus (11q23) is listed; CD3E is not among the reported **MG** risk genes as of the latest catalog releases. ClinGen/GenCC curation supports CD3E as a bona fide human disease gene for T cell immunodeficiency due to loss-of-function, but there is no curated gene–disease validity relationship for autoimmune **MG**; thus, rare coding CD3E variation is established for immunodeficiency, not **MG**. Narrative and systematic reviews of **MG** genetics emphasize HLA and several immune-regulatory loci (e.g., PTPN22, CTLA4, TNFRSF11A, CHRNA1) and do not implicate CD3E, consistent with the absence of a clear human genetic anchor for CD3E in **MG**. CD3E is a core component of the T cell receptor (TCR)–CD3 complex and is required for TCR signaling; it participates in multiple Reactome immune pathways (TCR signaling, immunoregulatory interactions), placing it centrally in adaptive immunity relevant to **MG** pathogenesis. Expression profiling shows CD3E is highly and selectively expressed in T cells and thymus; GTEx reports strong expression in whole blood/spleen, and HPA single-cell/tissue data identify CD3E as a T cell marker, matching the T cell– and thymus–centric biology of **MG**. Human Protein Atlas reports CD3E as a canonical T-cell marker with high expression in thymus and peripheral T cells, supporting the relevant cell types implicated in **MG** (thymic hyperplasia and T-dependent autoantibody production). Immunopathogenesis reviews of **MG** highlight key roles for CD4+ T cells, thymic germinal centers, and T cell help in the production of pathogenic anti–acetylcholine receptor antibodies, providing strong mechanistic plausibility for TCR–CD3 pathway involvement (though not specifically CD3E by genetics). Orthologous mouse models (Cd3e) show that loss of CD3e disrupts T cell development and results in severe T cell deficiency, aligning with CD3E’s essential role in TCR signaling; while this supports pathway relevance, it does not model **MG** directly. Lines of evidence agree that CD3E is indispensable for T cell signaling and that **MG** is T cell–dependent; however, there is a lack of direct human genetic association between CD3E and **MG** and no published **MG**–CD3E eQTL/colocalization anchors. Animal and functional evidence support pathway-level plausibility but do not establish CD3E as the causal gene for **MG**. No explicit refuting human genetic studies have identified protective or risk associations at CD3E; the main limitation is absence of positive human genetic anchors. CD3E encodes the epsilon chain of the TCR–CD3 complex, with strong, specific expression in T cells and thymus and central placement in TCR signaling pathways (GTEx, HPA, Reactome). **MG** pathogenesis is T cell–dependent with thymic involvement, which provides coherent biological plausibility for CD3E pathway relevance (Berrih-Aknin 2014). However, curated human genetic resources report no genome-wide significant **MG** association at or near the CD3E locus, and there are no published **MG** risk variant–CD3E eQTL colocalizations or replicated rare variant burdens implicating CD3E (GWAS Catalog; ClinGen shows immunodeficiency, not **MG**, for CD3E). Mouse Cd3e loss-of-function models confirm an essential role in T cell development but do not model **MG**, so they support mechanism rather than causality. Direction of effect for **MG** risk with respect to CD3E remains undetermined in humans. Overall, the evidence supports biological coherence but lacks direct human genetic causation, yielding a weak causal, human-relevant link at present. Myasthenia gravis (trait-level catalog of associations) GWAS Catalog page lists **MG**-associated loci; CD3E locus not reported as associated. Gene-disease validity (human) ClinGen curation links CD3E to T-cell immunodeficiency; no curated link to **MG**. Myasthenia gravis (overview and genetics summary) Lancet review summarizing **MG** epidemiology, immunopathogenesis, and genetics; CD3E not highlighted among **MG** risk genes. T cell receptor signaling pathway CD3E participates in TCR signaling pathways relevant to adaptive immunity. Whole blood/spleen (bulk); immune tissues CD3E shows high expression in immune tissues; supports T cell relevance. Single-cell and tissue expression (human) HPA indicates CD3E is a T cell marker with high expression in thymus and peripheral T cells. Mouse Cd3e Gene summary and linked resources indicate knockout causes T cell deficiency; aligns with essential role in TCR signaling. **MG** immunopathogenesis (thymus, T cells) Review emphasizes T cell help and thymic involvement in **MG** pathogenesis. No genome-wide significant human genetic association connects CD3E to **MG** to date (GWAS Catalog). ClinGen/GenCC link CD3E to T cell immunodeficiency through loss-of-function, not to autoimmunity. CD3E’s high expression in T cells and thymus and its central role in TCR signaling (GTEx, HPA, Reactome) align with **MG** biology, but these are pathway-level plausibility lines rather than causal anchors. Mouse Cd3e knockouts show T cell deficiency, confirming function but not **MG** causality. Direction of effect for **MG** risk with respect to CD3E is unknown in humans. Overall, biological coherence is moderate, functional/animal support is indirect, and human genetic causality is currently lacking; thus the integrated evidence supports a Weak verdict. ## CD40-D009157

**Drug keywords:** mg

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

> CD40 Myasthenia Gravis Weak Across curated GWAS overviews for myasthenia gravis (**MG**), CD40 is not reported as a reproducible genome-wide significant locus; prominent **MG** loci include HLA and several immune regulators, but CD40 is not among the leading signals. NHGRI-EBI GWAS Catalog listings for **MG** do not currently include a genome-wide significant association at the CD40 locus (20q13.12). No **MG**-specific rare-variant burden or loss-of-function association has been reported for CD40 in large exome aggregation resources; public GeneBass gene page shows constraint and general associations but no **MG**-specific burden signal. CD40 is a TNF receptor family costimulatory receptor; engagement by CD40L (CD154) activates NF-κB and promotes B-cell proliferation, germinal center formation, and class-switch recombination—core processes for high-affinity IgG autoantibody generation. CD40 shows high expression in human lymphoid tissues and on B cells, dendritic cells, and monocytes, supporting relevant cell types for **MG** autoantibody pathophysiology. GTEx expression confirms CD40 expression in immune-relevant tissues (spleen, whole blood, EBV-transformed B cells), consistent with a humoral autoimmunity role. Thymic pathology in AChR-positive **MG** features ectopic germinal centers with abundant B cells and APCs expressing costimulatory molecules; CD40–CD40L interactions are highlighted as key components of these germinal center reactions in **MG** thymus. Single-cell transcriptomic atlas of human thymus identifies mature B cells and dendritic cell subsets expressing costimulatory machinery compatible with CD40 biology, supporting the thymus as a plausible site of action in **MG**. Network context places CD40 with CD40LG, TRAF adaptors, and NF-κB pathway components, aligning with pathways known to drive autoreactive B-cell activation. Cd40 knockout mice and human CD40 deficiency display profound defects in germinal center formation and class-switched antibody production (hyper-IgM phenotype), supporting a critical role in generating pathogenic IgG autoantibodies (mechanistically relevant to **MG**). Mouse Genome Informatics (MGI) curation of Cd40 indicates essential roles in adaptive immune responses and germinal center biology; orthology to human CD40 is strong. **MG** pathogenesis in animal models (EAMG) is antibody-mediated and depends on T cell help and germinal centers; this indirectly implicates CD40 signaling, though we did not identify a definitive **MG**-specific Cd40 perturbation study meeting strict criteria here. Evidence is mechanistically coherent: CD40 drives germinal center and class-switched antibody responses; **MG** features thymic ectopic GCs and pathogenic IgG autoantibodies, and CD40 is expressed in the relevant immune cells. However, there is no clear, replicated human genetic association of CD40 with **MG**, and direct **MG** animal-perturbation evidence for CD40 is limited. No explicit refuting genetic or functional studies were identified. CD40 biology is highly consistent with the humoral autoimmune mechanisms that underlie myasthenia gravis. It is strongly expressed in B cells, dendritic cells and monocytes, and its signaling via CD40L drives germinal center formation, class switching and high-affinity IgG production—processes central to AChR-antibody–mediated **MG** (Reactome; HPA; GTEx). The **MG** thymus exhibits ectopic germinal centers and abundant costimulatory signals, with reviews emphasizing the involvement of CD40–CD40L interactions in these lesions (Berrih-Aknin 2014; Park et al. 2020). Animal and human loss-of-function data for CD40 support a necessary role in germinal center and IgG responses, aligning with **MG** pathophysiology, though direct Cd40-perturbation in **MG** models is not clearly established (IMPC; MGI). Critically, robust human genetic anchors (GWAS, fine-mapping/coloc, or rare-variant burden) linking CD40 to **MG** are lacking in curated resources (GWAS Catalog; genetics overview). Altogether, the mechanistic and cellular evidence supports plausibility and suggests that increased CD40 signaling would be expected to increase **MG** risk/severity, but the absence of strong human genetic evidence keeps the causal inference at a weak level. However, we did not identify robust, replicated **MG**-specific human genetic associations for CD40 (GWAS or rare variants). Animal and human loss-of-function data for CD40 support necessity for germinal center/IgG responses, aligning with **MG**’s autoantibody mechanism, but direct **MG** model perturbations of CD40 were not firmly established here. Therefore, the integrated causal evidence score is weak. Key sources: Gilhus et al. Nat Rev Dis Primers 2019 (genetics/pathogenesis), Berrih-Aknin et al. Single-cell profiling of human breast tumors identifies CD8A as a defining marker of cytotoxic T cell clusters; the abundance and activation state of CD8A+ T cells vary across tumors and relate to immune phenotypes in triple-negative breast cancer. Across cancers including breast, higher CD8+ T cell infiltration (captured by CD8A and related CTL gene signatures) is associated with improved patient survival, supporting a protective role of CTL activity in tumor control. In triple-negative breast cancer, enrichment of cytotoxic/CD8A+ tissue-resident memory T cells is associated with significantly improved relapse-free and ***overall survival***, implicating heightened CD8A+ T cell activity as beneficial in disease course. CD8A participates in T cell receptor signaling and co-receptor function with MHC class I, situating it mechanistically in cytotoxic T cell activation pathways that mediate anti-tumor immunity. Cd8a knockout mice (strong one-to-one ortholog) lack CD8+ T cells and exhibit impaired cytotoxic T cell responses, demonstrating that Cd8a is necessary for CTL function—mechanistically aligning with the human observation that CD8A+ infiltration restrains tumors. International Mouse Phenotyping Consortium resources document immune system phenotypes for Cd8a null mice (loss of CD8+ T cells), supporting conserved function essential for anti-tumor cytotoxic immunity....

No curated gene–disease validity assertions connect CFD to **MG**; OMIM and GenCC list CFD primarily for complement factor D deficiency/immunodeficiency, not **MG**. CFD encodes complement factor D, the essential serine protease of the alternative pathway (AP) that cleaves factor B in the C3bB complex to generate the AP C3 convertase; thus CFD activity is required for AP amplification of complement. Reactome places CFD in the Complement cascade and specifically in the alternative pathway amplification loop, which potentiates C3/C5 activation downstream of antibody-triggered complement. In AChR-antibody–positive myasthenia gravis, complement-mediated postsynaptic damage at the neuromuscular junction is a key pathogenic mechanism; the classical pathway is initiated by IgG1/IgG3 and is amplified by the AP, giving a biologically plausible route for CFD to modulate disease...

**Match 2:**
> ...is a key pathogenic mechanism; the classical pathway is initiated by IgG1/IgG3 and is amplified by the AP, giving a biologically plausible route for CFD to modulate disease severity. The complement AP’s role as an amplification loop is well established, with CFD indispensable for AP convertase formation; increased CFD activity is expected to increase complement amplification, whereas reduced CFD activity would decrease amplification. CFD shows predominant expression in hepatocytes/adipose tissue and in some innate immune cell types and is secreted into plasma, consistent with a systemic complement protein that can act at the neuromuscular junction where complement deposition/damage occurs in **MG**. Protein-level resources report CFD as a secreted plasma protein with high expression in liver/adipose and detectable in myeloid cells, consistent with its role in systemic alternative pathway activation. Experimental autoimmune myasthenia gravis (EAMG) models show complement-dependent neuromuscular junction injury; terminal complement activity is necessary for full disease expression. While this implicates complement broadly, CFD-specific knockout/perturbation evidence in **MG** models was not identified. Evidence is directionally coherent at the mechanistic level: **MG** pathology involves complement-mediated NMJ injury and the AP amplifies this response, with CFD essential for AP activity. However, there is no direct human genetic association of CFD with **MG**, nor reported colocalization of **MG** risk with CFD molecular QTLs. No explicit refuting genetic studies were found; the main limitation is absence of direct human-variant evidence for CFD. There is no direct human genetic association linking CFD to myasthenia gravis in GWAS Catalog or Open Targets Genetics, and curated gene–disease validity resources do not connect CFD to **MG**. Nevertheless, pathophysiology of AChR-antibody–positive **MG** involves complement-mediated postsynaptic damage, and the alternative pathway functions as an amplification loop that requires complement factor D. Thus, increased CFD activity would be expected to enhance complement amplification at the neuromuscular junction, whereas decreased CFD would be expected to reduce it. Tissue and secretion profiles (liver/adipose/innate immune cells; plasma) are compatible with systemic delivery of complement proteins to the NMJ. Animal models support the complement dependency of EAMG but do not provide CFD-specific manipulations. Overall, the causal link remains inferential—biologically plausible and consistent with disease mechanisms but lacking direct human genetic anchors—supporting a Weak causal, human-relevant relationship. ## CFTR-D001987

There is very strong human genetic evidence that loss-of-function in CFTR causes a syndrome (cystic fibrosis) in which bronchiectasis is a defining pulmonary phenotype, establishing directionality that decreased CFTR activity increases bronchiectasis risk/severity (PMID:27140670). ClinGen’s gene–disease validity further supports CFTR involvement in CFTR-related disorders encompassing isolated bronchiectasis outside classic CF presentations (ClinGen HGNC:1884). Population-level data indicate heterozygous CFTR carriers have elevated risk of bronchiectasis, consistent with a **dose**–response relationship between CFTR function and bronchiectasis susceptibility (PMID:31940058). Functional genomics highlight airway epithelial cells—especially CFTR-rich ionocytes—as key cells of action, integrating tissue localization with mechanism of impaired mucociliary clearance (PMIDs:30069044, 30069046; GTEx: PMIDs:32913098). Large-animal CFTR-null models (pig, ferret) reproduce airway infection, mucus obstruction, and structural remodeling consistent with bronchiectasis, reinforcing causality and direction of effect (PMIDs:20581002, 20054395). While mouse lungs incompletely model bronchiectasis, cross-species alignment and human genetics provide a coherent, human-relevant causal relationship. Cystic fibrosis; bronchiectasis pathophysiology Authoritative review summarizing CF lung disease and bronchiectasis as hallmark phenotype; used for phenotypic linkage and mechanistic context. CFTR-related disorders (includes bronchiectasis)...

> ...placement. CFTR-null pig; lung Sci Transl Med 2010: CF pigs develop airway disease with infection and airway remodeling consistent with bronchiectasis. CFTR-null ferret; lung Nature/early ferret model reports: CF ferrets exhibit airway disease with features consistent with bronchiectasis. Lung/airway epithelium Human Protein Atlas: CFTR protein/RNA expression in airway epithelium. Convergent human genetic evidence supports a causal link between CFTR dysfunction and bronchiectasis. Biallelic CFTR pathogenic variants (cystic fibrosis) lead to bronchiectasis as a core pulmonary manifestation (PMID:27140670), and ClinGen classifies CFTR-related disorder—encompassing bronchiectasis—as definitively associated with CFTR (ClinGen). Population data show elevated bronchiectasis risk among CFTR heterozygotes (PMID:31940058), supporting a **dose**–response direction of effect in humans (decreased CFTR function increases bronchiectasis risk/severity). Functional genomics place CFTR in airway epithelial cells, especially CFTR-high ionocytes (PMIDs:30069044, 30069046; GTEx), providing mechanistic coherence with mucociliary clearance failure leading to infection and airway wall damage. Large-animal CFTR-null models (pig, ferret) recapitulate key elements of CF-like lung disease and structural changes consistent with bronchiectasis (PMIDs:20581002, 20054395). Some earlier small cohorts reported mixed enrichment of CFTR variants in idiopathic bronchiectasis, but the weight of current genetics and functional evidence is strongly consistent. Overall ICES=5.4 (Very Strong).

## CLCN1-D009157

**Drug keywords:** mg

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

> CLCN1 Myasthenia Gravis Insufficient Across curated GWAS resources for myasthenia gravis, no common-variant association or fine-mapped signal implicating CLCN1 has been reported; established **MG** loci concentrate in HLA and immune-regulatory genes, with CLCN1 absent from reported **MG** risk loci. Open Targets Genetics does not prioritize CLCN1 for **MG** via credible sets or V2G linking (as of latest releases), indicating lack of locus-level support for CLCN1 in **MG**. Gene-centric burden/PheWAS resources show CLCN1 is linked to myotonia phenotypes from rare variants but do not report association to **MG**, supporting a different disease mechanism for CLCN1. Curated gene–disease validity assigns CLCN1 as causative for myotonia congenita (skeletal muscle channelopathy), not **MG**, underscoring absence of a recognized **MG** genetic relationship. CLCN1 is strongly and specifically expressed in skeletal muscle at the mRNA level, aligning with its known role in muscle excitability rather than immune regulation. Protein expression profiling shows skeletal muscle–enriched CLCN1, with limited/absent expression in immune tissues such as blood and thymus, arguing against primary immune-cell–mediated disease relevance. Mechanistic reviews establish CLCN1 (ClC-1) as the principal skeletal muscle chloride channel controlling membrane excitability; dysfunction causes myotonia, not autoimmune neuromuscular junction transmission defects. Authoritative **MG** pathogenesis reviews list autoantigens and genetic risk concentrated in immune and neuromuscular junction genes (e.g., AChR, MuSK, LRP4), with no mention of CLCN1 as an **MG** autoantigen or genetic risk factor. Mouse Clcn1 mutants/knockouts consistently exhibit myotonia and muscle hyperexcitability phenotypes, not autoimmune **MG**-like weakness or thymic/immune phenotypes, indicating phenotype class mismatch with **MG**. Functional and comparative reviews of ClC-1 support its conserved role in skeletal muscle excitability; no animal data demonstrate induction or modulation of **MG**-like autoimmunity by altering Clcn1. All lines of evidence converge on a lack of causal linkage: no **MG** human genetic association for CLCN1, strong tissue specificity to muscle rather than immune cells, and animal models recapitulate myotonia but not **MG**. **MG** pathophysiology centers on autoimmunity to neuromuscular junction components; CLCN1 is not an established autoantigen. No credible refuting study demonstrates a direct CLCN1→**MG** risk effect. There is no human genetic anchor supporting CLCN1 as a causal risk gene for myasthenia gravis: GWAS resources and credible set prioritizations for **MG** do not implicate CLCN1 (GWAS Catalog; Open Targets Genetics). Curated gene–disease validity and gene-burden resources consistently tie CLCN1 to myotonia congenita, not **MG** (ClinGen; GeneBass). Functionally, CLCN1 is a skeletal muscle–specific chloride channel essential for muscle excitability (GTEx; Human Protein Atlas; Physiol Rev), whereas **MG** is an autoimmune neuromuscular junction disorder with different molecular autoantigens and immune risk architecture (Nat Rev Dis Primers). Rodent Clcn1 models show myotonia without autoimmune or neuromuscular junction antibody–mediated pathology (MGI; Physiol Rev). Direction of effect and relevant immune cell types linking CLCN1 to **MG** cannot be inferred. Collectively, the evidence is insufficient to support a causal, human-relevant CLCN1→**MG** relationship. Myasthenia gravis (trait-level GWAS catalog) Curated associations for **MG**; no reported signals implicating CLCN1. Myasthenia gravis (credible sets and V2G) No credible sets prioritize CLCN1 as a causal gene for **MG**. Large-scale human genetics of blood traits prioritize the CSF3R locus for neutrophil and white blood cell traits, placing the gene centrally in human granulopoiesis; while these are not **drug**-related outcomes, they provide biological coherence for mechanisms underlying many **drug**-related hematologic adverse events. Csf3r knockout mice exhibit profound neutropenia and impaired neutrophil production, establishing directionality that decreased CSF3R activity lowers neutrophil counts; although not an adverse **drug** phenotype, it aligns with a key mechanistic axis for hematologic toxicities. Independent mouse genetic resources report that Csf3r disruption leads to abnormal granulocyte/neutrophil numbers, corroborating the role of CSF3R in myelopoiesis in vivo. Across functional genomics and animal models, evidence consistently supports CSF3R as a critical regulator of neutrophil development and function, with decreased activity leading to neutropenia and increased signaling linked to neutrophilia. However, there is a notable gap: human genetic studies explicitly linking CSF3R variation to **drug**-related adverse reactions are not identified in curated GWAS sources. Thus, mechanistic coherence is high, but indication-specific human genetic anchoring is lacking. CSF3R is a well-established regulator of granulopoiesis, with strong expression in neutrophils and clear signaling mechanisms (JAK/STAT, MAPK) supporting its central role in neutrophil biology. Human genetics robustly implicates the CSF3R locus in neutrophil and white blood cell traits, indicating human relevance for hematologic homeostasis. Animal knockout studies demonstrate that loss of CSF3R function causes severe neutropenia, establishing direction of effect for neutrophil count. Despite this mechanistic and cross-species coherence, curated human genetics resources do not presently show associations between CSF3R and **drug**-related side effects or adverse reactions per se. Therefore, while CSF3R’s biology plausibly intersects with pathways underlying certain adverse reactions (e.g., hematologic toxicity), the absence of direct, indication-specific human genetic anchors leads to an overall assessment of insufficient evidence for a causal CSF3R–**drug**-adverse-reaction relationship at this time. Curated Mendelian resource documents that FH-I is caused by a CYP11B1/CYP11B2 chimeric gene at 8q24.3, providing replicated, family-based genetic evidence linking the CYP11B1 locus to hyperaldosteronism via promoter swap. GeneReviews summarizes multiple independent families with FH-I due to a CYP11B1–CYP11B2 chimeric gene; aldosterone production becomes ACTH-regulated because of CYP11B1 promoter control, establishing a human genetic mechanism connecting the CYP11B1 locus to hyperaldosteronism. By contrast, biallelic loss-of-function variants in CYP11B1 cause congenital adrenal hyperplasia due to...

> ...mechanism connecting the CYP11B1 locus to hyperaldosteronism. By contrast, biallelic loss-of-function variants in CYP11B1 cause congenital adrenal hyperplasia due to 11β-hydroxylase deficiency, characterized by excess 11‑deoxycorticosterone and often low/suppressed aldosterone (mineralocorticoid hypertension but not primary hyperaldosteronism), clarifying that decreased CYP11B1 enzymatic activity is not a cause of hyperaldosteronism. CYP11B1 is highly and specifically expressed in adrenal cortex tissue, consistent with a role in adrenal steroidogenesis and providing the appropriate tissue context for promoter-driven misexpression mechanisms. CYP11B1 encodes steroid 11β-hydroxylase, a mitochondrial P450 in cortisol/corticosterone biosynthesis. Its close paralog CYP11B2 encodes aldosterone synthase; the two genes lie in tandem, facilitating unequal ***crossover*** and formation of the pathogenic chimeric gene underlying FH-I. Across lines, mechanistic and animal evidence plausibly link reduced FAAH activity (increased endocannabinoid tone) to sleep promotion, but large human genetic studies of insomnia and related traits have not implicated FAAH. No strong refuting human genetics were found—rather, a consistent absence of genome-wide signals—so the overall picture is biologically coherent but lacks human-genetic anchoring and clear DoE in humans. FAAH degrades somnogenic fatty acid amides, notably anandamide and oleamide; its loss or reduction increases endocannabinoid tone (Nature 1996; PNAS 2001, 2004). **Match 2:**
> ...endocannabinoid tone promotes sleep; integrates FAAH’s role. Human-genetic anchors for FAAH in insomnia are currently lacking: large GWAS/meta-analyses for insomnia symptoms and sleep duration do not report FAAH among significant loci (Jansen 2019; Lane 2019; Dashti 2019). The best-established human variant (FAAH C385A) clearly reduces enzyme function and increases endocannabinoid levels (Chiang 2004), and a human FAAH-OUT regulatory deletion further underscores that decreased FAAH elevates endocannabinoid tone (Habib 2019). Functionally, FAAH is the primary degradative enzyme for anandamide and oleamide (Cravatt 1996), and these lipids are somnogenic in animal studies (e.g., oleamide in Science 1995), with reviews summarizing region- and **dose**-dependent sleep promotion by endocannabinoid signaling (Murillo-Rodríguez 2008). Nevertheless, direct causal human evidence linking FAAH variation to insomnia risk is insufficient. Thus, while the biology is coherent and animal/biochemical evidence suggest reduced FAAH activity could promote sleep, the T–I link remains weak without strong human genetic association.

Gpr40 (Ffar1) knockout/deficiency reduces fatty-acid potentiation of GSIS and can impair in vivo insulin secretion; effects on glucose tolerance vary by model and context. Overall, the mechanistic role in insulin secretion is consistent, while causal impact on diabetes risk in humans lacks genetic anchors. FFAR1 (GPR40) is a Gq/11-coupled long-chain fatty acid receptor enriched in pancreatic beta cells, where it potentiates glucose-stimulated insulin secretion via PLC/Ca2+ signaling (Briscoe 2003; human islet studies). This provides a biologically coherent mechanism linking FFAR1 activity to glycemic control and insulin secretion in relevant cells (HPA; Segerstolpe 2016). However, large-scale human genetic studies (fine-mapped GWAS and exome aggregation) have not identified FFAR1 as a T2D risk gene, and islet eQTL colocalization studies have not prioritized FFAR1 at diabetes loci (Mahajan 2018; Vujkovic 2020; Miguel-Escalada 2019). In mouse models, FFAR1 modulates fatty-acid–potentiated insulin secretion, but the direction of whole-body phenotypes varies across knockout and overexpression systems, indicating context- and **dose**-dependent effects (Steneberg 2005; Latour/Diabetes 2007; Nagasumi 2009). Taken together, there is strong mechanistic plausibility and functional support for FFAR1 in insulin secretion, but limited human-genetic causality for diabetes risk per se. We therefore judge the causal, human-relevant relationship between FFAR1 and diabetes as moderate: biologically compelling yet without convergent human genetic anchors. In contrast, there is strong biological coherence: FFAR1 is expressed in human beta cells (HPA; Segerstolpe 2016), is activated by long-chain fatty acids via Gq/PLC/Ca2+ (Briscoe 2003), and potentiates insulin secretion in human islets (Flodgren 2007). Animal models consistently indicate a role in fatty-acid–potentiated insulin secretion but show mixed whole-animal glycemic phenotypes, likely reflecting **dose**/context effects (Steneberg 2005; Latour 2007; Nagasumi 2009). Direction of effect from functional data suggests that decreased FFAR1 activity would reduce GSIS and could worsen glycemic control. Given strong mechanistic but weak human-genetic evidence, we assign a Moderate causal verdict (ICES 3.5).

General frameworks of adverse **drug** reaction biology emphasize immune HLA loci and **drug** metabolism/transport pathways as primary genetic determinants; GNRHR does not belong to these canonical mechanism classes. Mouse models with disruption of Gnrhr display reproductive axis phenotypes (hypogonadism/infertility), aligning with endocrine roles rather than **drug** adverse reaction biology; no aligned animal evidence links Gnrhr alteration to generalized susceptibility to adverse **drug** reactions. Cross-species phenotype mappings (Monarch/OMIM-derived) associate GNRHR with reproductive phenotypes, not adverse **drug** reaction classes. Across human genetics (GWAS Catalog, gene-disease validity), functional genomics (GTEx/HPA, UniProt pathway annotations), and model organism data (IMPC/Monarch), evidence converges on endocrine reproductive axis biology for GNRHR with no replicated signals for adverse **drug** reactions; no explicit refutations were identified, but the available evidence does not support a causal link to ADR. Large-scale curated human genetics resources show no reported associations between GNRHR variants and adverse **drug** reaction phenotypes (NHGRI-EBI GWAS Catalog), and gene–disease validity efforts curate only reproductive phenotypes for this gene (GenCC/ClinGen). Canonical mechanistic axes underlying many adverse **drug** reactions emphasize immune (HLA) and xenobiotic metabolism/transport pathways rather than pituitary GPCR signaling (reviewed by Pirmohamed 2014). Model organism data (IMPC/Monarch) similarly align Gnrhr perturbation with reproductive phenotypes, not **drug**-related adverse reactions. Together, these lines of evidence indicate insufficient support for a causal, human-relevant relationship between GNRHR and generalized **drug**-related side effects or adverse reactions. > HTT Huntington Disease Very Strong A CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in exon 1 of HTT is the pathogenic mutation causing autosomal dominant Huntington disease; the expansion is unstable and segregates with disease in families. Clinical genetics curation classifies HTT–Huntington disease as a definitive gene–disease relationship. Authoritative catalog summarizes that repeats ≥40 are fully penetrant, 36–39 reduced penetrance, and <36 non-pathogenic, consolidating decades of linkage and molecular studies. Genetic modifier studies show that DNA repair pathway variation (e.g., FAN1, MSH3, MLH1/MLH3) robustly modifies age at onset, supporting a mechanism of somatic CAG expansion driving human disease. Pure CAG tract length, not polyglutamine length per se, best predicts human age at onset; interruptions that reduce the pure CAG tract delay onset, reinforcing a repeat-expansion–driven toxic mechanism. Modifier genetics in DNA repair genes and evidence of somatic expansion in striatum add mechanistic clarity and align with the neuron-centric phenotype (PMID:26232222; PMID:31199589; PMID:30643253). Expression of HTT in human brain neurons (GTEx; HPA) and robust animal models with expanded alleles that recapitulate HD-like phenotypes further reinforce biological plausibility and directionality, while Htt knockout lethality and LoF constraint demonstrate that disease is not haploinsufficiency (PMID:7727705; 10.1038/s41586-020-2308-7). No credible alternative gene explains HD. The integrated evidence warrants a Very Strong verdict.

## IL6R-D009157

**Drug keywords:** mg

**Category:** Non-therapeutic or drug development context

> IL6R Myasthenia Gravis Weak Across curated **MG** GWAS in the GWAS Catalog, genome-wide significant risk loci include HLA region and several immune-regulatory genes, but no associations map to the IL6R locus (1q21.3); IL6R is not reported as a prioritized gene for **MG** signals. Open Targets Genetics (variant-to-gene/credible set framework) does not prioritize IL6R for **MG**; no **MG** credible sets colocalize with IL6R cis-QTLs as of the latest release. IL6R encodes the interleukin-6 receptor alpha chain; upon IL-6 binding, it forms a signaling complex with IL6ST (gp130) to activate JAK/STAT3 and related cascades central to adaptive immune responses (B-cell maturation, T cell effector programs). GTEx shows IL6R mRNA expression in immune-relevant tissues (e.g., whole blood, spleen) and relatively low expression in skeletal muscle, consistent with a primarily hematopoietic/immune cell role rather than at the neuromuscular junction. Human Protein Atlas reports IL6R protein/mRNA enriched in immune cell types (e.g., subsets of B and T cells and monocytes), aligning with autoimmune mechanisms operative in **MG** (autoantibody production driven by T–B cell interactions). **MG** is an autoantibody-mediated autoimmune disease with key roles for T and B cells and, in early-onset **MG**, thymic germinal centers—mechanistic contexts in which IL-6/IL6R signaling is known to modulate B cell differentiation and T helper programs. No robust IL6R-targeted genetic manipulation studies (e.g., Il6r knockout/knock-in or receptor-specific perturbations) demonstrating causal modification of experimental autoimmune myasthenia gravis (EAMG) were identified in curated animal model databases; available cytokine-manipulation data are either non-specific to IL6R or outside **MG**. Evidence lines are sparse and largely inferential: human genetics provides no direct IL6R–**MG** association; functional genomics and pathway data support immune-cell plausibility; **MG** immunopathology involves T–B cell interactions consistent with IL-6 biology. No strong refuting human genetic evidence exists, but absence of GWAS/rare variant signals and lack of **MG**-specific IL6R animal-genetic data limit causal inference and leave the direction of effect unestablished. Direct human genetic anchors connecting IL6R to myasthenia gravis are currently lacking: **MG** GWAS do not implicate the IL6R locus, and no rare-variant burden signals have been reported for IL6R in **MG**. Nonetheless, IL6R is robustly expressed in immune cells and operates in the IL-6→gp130→JAK/STAT3 pathway, which shapes B-cell maturation and T cell effector programs relevant to autoantibody-mediated diseases, including **MG**. This yields good biological plausibility for IL6R as a modulator of **MG** immune circuits, particularly within lymphoid tissues and possibly thymic germinal centers. However, the absence of fine-mapped **MG** associations, colocalized QTL evidence at IL6R, or **MG**-focused IL6R genetic perturbations in animal models prevents establishing a causal, human-relevant link or a confident direction of effect. Overall, we judge the causal evidence as Weak: mechanistically coherent but without human genetic causality or decisive **MG**-specific functional/animal proof. Myasthenia gravis (GWAS trait page) No **MG** associations reported at the IL6R locus (1q21.3) among genome-wide significant hits. IL6R across traits (OT Genetics) No **MG** credible sets or colocalizations implicating IL6R as of latest release. IL-6 signaling pathway Curated pathway for IL6/IL6R–gp130 signaling and downstream JAK/STAT3. Expression (bulk tissue) IL6R expression enriched in immune-related tissues; low in skeletal muscle. Protein/mRNA expression (immune cell types) Immune cell-enriched IL6R expression profile. Myasthenia gravis (disease overview) Authoritative overview of **MG** immunopathology (autoantibody-mediated; T–B cell involvement). Mouse genetics (Il6r), general No curated Il6r genetic perturbation studies tied to EAMG identified; evidence non-informative for **MG**. Mouse phenotyping (Il6r), general Il6r phenotypes cataloged but not linked to EAMG; non-informative for **MG** causality. Summary: There is currently no direct human genetic evidence implicating IL6R in myasthenia gravis (**MG**): GWAS Catalog **MG** studies do not report signals at the IL6R locus, and Open Targets Genetics does not prioritize IL6R for **MG**. By contrast, biological coherence is reasonable: IL6R is an immune cell receptor initiating IL-6→gp130→JAK/STAT3 signaling (Reactome), with expression enriched in blood/immune tissues (GTEx, HPA). These pathways regulate T–B cell interactions and effector programs that underlie autoantibody production central to **MG** pathogenesis (disease overview review). However, we did not identify **MG**-focused IL6R genetic perturbation in animal models or colocalized molecular QTL evidence that would establish causality and direction of effect. On balance, the integrated evidence is Weak: mechanistically plausible with immune-cell alignment but lacking causal human genetics and **MG**-specific functional/animal anchors. Key sources: GWAS Catalog (EFO_0000700), Open Targets Genetics (IL6R gene page), Reactome R-HSA-1059683, GTEx IL6R, Human Protein Atlas IL6R, and a Myasthenia Gravis disease review.

Animal models do not recapitulate cHL but confirm essential hematopoietic roles of Jak2. A caveat is potential co-selection for PD-L1/PD-L2 within the amplicon; however, the weight of evidence indicates that JAK2 gain is itself contributory. Overall, multiple orthogonal lines converge on a causal, human-relevant relationship best explained by increased JAK2 dosage/activity in HRS cells driving cHL pathobiology. > TNFRSF17 Myasthenia Gravis Weak Across curated GWAS sources, TNFRSF17 is not listed among genome-wide significant loci for myasthenia gravis; the TNFRSF17 gene page in GWAS Catalog shows associations with other traits but not **MG** (no TNFRSF17→**MG** entry). The GWAS Catalog trait page for myasthenia gravis lists multiple risk loci, but none implicate TNFRSF17 as the causal gene via fine-mapping in the curated entries. Open Targets Genetics disease page for myasthenia gravis does not prioritize TNFRSF17 via credible sets, colocalized QTLs, TWAS or PWAS in their latest curated integration. TNFRSF17 encodes BCMA, a receptor for the B-cell survival ligands BAFF (TNFSF13B) and APRIL (TNFSF13); BCMA signaling supports plasma cell survival and antibody production, providing a mechanistic route to sustain autoantibody-secreting cells. Human Protein Atlas shows TNFRSF17 expression is highly enriched in plasmablasts/plasma cells (single-cell) and in bone marrow/lymphoid compartments (bulk), aligning with the known plasma-cell restricted expression of BCMA. **MG** is an antibody-mediated autoimmune disease; pathogenic autoantibodies (e.g., anti-AChR) are produced by B-lineage cells, including plasmablasts/plasma cells that can be found in thymic germinal centers and peripheral lymphoid tissues in **MG**—cellular contexts where BCMA biology is active. General protein function resources corroborate that BCMA is a TNF receptor superfamily member transmitting survival signals to plasma cells via NF-κB/MAPK pathways, consistent with persistence of antibody responses. Genetic ablation of Bcma in mice impairs the maintenance of long-lived bone-marrow plasma cells and long-term humoral immunity, demonstrating a causal role for BCMA in sustaining antibody-secreting cells (orthology strong for TNFRSF17↔Tnfrsf17). Mouse resource portals (MGI/IMPC) report that Tnfrsf17 knockout mice are viable with immune phenotypes centered on humoral response deficits, aligning with BCMA’s role in plasma-cell biology (phenotype class: decreased humoral immune response). No **MG**-specific genetic model evidence (e.g., Tnfrsf17 manipulation in experimental autoimmune **MG**) was identified in curated databases; thus animal evidence supports mechanism but not disease specificity. Biological coherence is high: **MG** is autoantibody-driven and TNFRSF17/BCMA sustains plasma cells that produce antibodies. Functional genomics shows plasma-cell-restricted expression compatible with disease tissues. Animal genetics confirms BCMA’s necessity for long-lived plasma cells. However, human genetic anchors (GWAS/fine-mapping, rare burden, TWAS) linking TNFRSF17 to **MG** are lacking in curated resources. No explicit refuting genetic studies identified; the main limitation is absence of direct human genetic association. TNFRSF17 encodes BCMA, a BAFF/APRIL receptor essential for plasma-cell survival and sustained antibody production (Mackay & Schneider 2009; UniProt). In myasthenia gravis, pathogenic autoantibodies drive disease, and plasmablasts/plasma cells in thymic germinal centers and peripheral lymphoid sites are key cellular actors (Gilhus 2016 NEJM). Functional genomics shows TNFRSF17 is strongly enriched in plasma cells, pinpointing plausible cells of action (Human Protein Atlas). In vivo, Bcma knockout diminishes long-lived plasma cells and humoral memory in mice, supporting the direction that reduced BCMA activity would reduce autoantibody persistence (O’Connor et al. 2004; MGI). Despite this mechanistic fit, curated human genetic datasets (GWAS Catalog and Open Targets Genetics) do not implicate TNFRSF17 in **MG** via fine-mapped loci, colocalized QTLs, TWAS or rare-variant burden.
